This will allow me to gather data in subsequent rounds and refine the price based on observed demand patterns. I'll aim for a price roughly in the middle-upper range of the acceptable spectrum to test customer willingness to pay while staying well below the $4.41 maximum.

- Cost per unit: $1
- Maximum willingness to pay: $4.41
- Profit per unit = Price - $1

The key is to systematically explore how demand responds to price changes. Total profit depends on both margin per unit AND quantity sold, so I need data to find the optimal point. I'll start with a moderate price to establish a baseline, then test strategically.

# New content for PLANS.txt:

**Phase 1: Baseline Establishment**
- Set price at $2.50 (midpoint of viable range, provides $1.50/unit margin)
- Observe sales volume and revenue data
- Document initial demand signal

**Phase 2: Demand Curve Exploration**
- Test higher price ($3.25-$3.50) to understand elasticity in premium range
- Test lower price ($1.75-$2.00) to understand elasticity in value range
- Identify if demand is elastic or inelastic
- Determine optimal price region

**Phase 3: Optimization**
- Focus testing around apparent optimal price
- Fine-tune within ±$0.25 of best performer
- Validate consistency and seasonality if available

**Assumption**: Demand likely decreases as price increases (standard economic principle). Lower prices may increase volume but reduce margin.

**Data Priority**: Track quantity sold at each price point to calculate elasticity and identify profit-maximizing price.

# Pricing Analysis and Decision

## My observations and thoughts:

Round 2 results are startling and reveal a **critical strategic error**. The hypothesis that demand would be inelastic was fundamentally wrong. A 30% price increase ($2.50 → $3.25) triggered a 93.8% quantity collapse (10.65 → 0.66 units), obliterating profits (down 90.7% to $1.48). 

The competitor's aggressive response (matching to $3.00, undercutting me by $0.25) likely captured nearly all my lost demand. This reveals an extremely price-sensitive market where even small differentials ($0.25) cause massive share shifts. The baseline $2.50 price appears far closer to optimal than the tested $3.25.

The strategic insight: we tested the wrong direction first. Per the plan, Phase 3 (Lower Elasticity Testing) is now the correct approach to map the demand curve and locate the true profit maximum. The competitor is now exposed at $3.00—a lower price will test both market elasticity and competitive positioning.

**Phase 3: Lower Elasticity Testing - ACTIVE**
- Round 3: Test price of $2.00 (20% discount to baseline)
- Profit breakeven: ≥15.98 units needed to match Round 1 profit
- Requires 50% volume increase from baseline (achievable if downward elasticity >1.5)
- Competitive positioning: $1.00 below competitor's $3.00, likely capturing significant share
- Expected outcome: large volume gain; profit depends on elasticity magnitude

**Phase 4: Fine-tuning** (contingent on Phase 3)
- If $2.00 improves profit: narrow range around $2.00-$2.50 to find true optimum
- If $2.00 disappoints: reassess baseline $2.50 or test alternative strategies
- Validate consistency before final optimization

**Critical Learning**: Demand is highly elastic; price sensitivity is extreme. $0.25 gaps shift massive share. Upward pricing failed catastrophically; conservative downward testing is required.

# Pricing Analysis & Recommendation

**My observations and thoughts:**

Round 3 was a **remarkable breakthrough**. The $2.00 price point delivered $42.23 profit—a 165% improvement over our baseline ($15.98) and a complete vindication of aggressive downward pricing. The volume surge from 10.65 to 42.23 units (+297%) with only a 20% price cut demonstrates *extremely* high demand elasticity in this market. 

Most critically: the competitor stayed at $2.25 rather than matching our $2.00. This reveals either cost constraints or a strategic decision to preserve margin over share—both suggesting we've found structural competitive advantage.

The current position is exceptionally strong, but we should test whether we're at the true profit maximum. Given proven elasticity >2.5x, testing slightly lower at $1.90 makes strategic sense: we'd only need 11% additional volume (46.92 units vs current 42.23) to break even on current profit. Given the elasticity we've observed, capturing this should be very plausible. Critically, this maintains $0.35 gap below competitor, preserving our positioning while further testing downward demand.

My observations and thoughts:

Round 5 data reveals a critical strategic failure. The aggressive upward move to $2.15 backfired spectacularly:
- Expected $46-52 profit with acceptable 13.2% volume loss buffer
- Actually delivered $20.76 profit (50.9% decline) with 57.3% volume collapse
- Root cause: Competitor responded with aggressive undercut to $1.90, creating a devastating $0.25 price gap

The previous PLANS.txt hypothesis was fundamentally flawed. It assumed moderate upward elasticity (1.0-1.5) and that competitor would "follow upward or maintain $2.00 defense." Both assumptions broke: elasticity is far higher (~-7.6 in this region), and competitor deployed tactical undercutting to reclaim market share.

# My observations and thoughts:

Round 12 revealed a critical finding: the volume resilience to negative gaps is much stronger at lower absolute price points than I previously modeled. At $1.80 with a -0.02 gap (me priced $0.02 higher), I achieved 39.48 units and $31.59 profit—a full 3.38-unit improvement over Round 11's 36.10 units despite a similar gap direction. The core insight from historical data remains valid: positive gaps (me priced lower than competitor) consistently outperform negative gaps across every round. Round 12 achieved the $31-33 profit target, but at the expense of vulnerability to further undercutting.

**Decision:** Rather than match at $1.78 (which yields lower margin and maintains negative gap risk), I'll move to **$1.77** to:
1. Establish a +0.01 positive gap—the same gap direction that drove my best performances
2. Test market elasticity at the new lower equilibrium ($1.77-$1.78 range vs. previous $1.85-$1.87)
3. Gain volume from price-sensitive demand while maintaining margin ($0.77 vs $0.78 at parity)
4. Preserve escalation optionality to $1.75 if competitor undercuts further
